Omniprediction via Proper Calibration and Multiaccuracy

To motivate proper calibration, we recall the notion of Decision OI introduced by
Gopalan et al., (2023)
.
We restate their definition and extend it to include the sequential prediction setting.

Definition 3.2

(Decision OI)
.

Fix a collection of loss functions
\mathcal{L}
.
For a predictor
p:\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
, the Decision OI error over a distribution
\mathcal{D}
is given by
\mathcal{L}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DecOIErr}_{\mathcal{D}}(p)
.

\mathcal{L}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DecOIErr}_{\mathcal{D}}(p)=\sup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}}\left|\E_{x,y\sim\mathcal{D}}[\ell(k_{\ell}\circ p(x),y)]-\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim\mathcal{D}\\
\tilde{y}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p(x))\end{subarray}}[\ell(k_{\ell}\circ p(x),\tilde{y})]\right|

For a sequence of
T
predictions
\mathbf{p}
and context, outcome pairs
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
, the Decision OI error is given by
\mathcal{L}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DecOIErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

\mathcal{L}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DecOIErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\ell(k_{\ell}\circ p_{t}(x_{t}),y_{t})-\E_{\tilde{y}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p_{t}(x_{t}))}\ell(k_{\ell}\circ p_{t}(x_{t}),\tilde{y}))\right|

As in all notions of outcome indistinguishability, Decision OI compares the expected value of some test on outcomes sampled from the “real” world (i.e.,
y_{t}
) versus the value on outcomes sampled from a “modeled” world, where outcomes are sampled according to our predictions
\tilde{y}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p_{t}(x_{t}))
.

Gopalan et al., (2023)
showed that Decision OI plus a certain multiaccuracy condition suffice for omniprediction; they use full
\ell_{1}
-calibration to achieve Decision OI.
We show that, for any loss class
\mathcal{L}
, proper calibration suffices to imply Decision OI.
In fact, Proper Calibration is equivalent to Decision OI for the class of all loss function
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{all}}=\{\ell:[0,1]\to[-1,1]\}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

For any predictor
p:\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
and distribution
\mathcal{D}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{all}}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DecOIErr}_{\mathcal{D}}(p)=\mathrm{PCalErr}_{\mathcal{D}}(p).

For every sequence of
T
predictions
\mathbf{p}
and context, outcome pairs
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{all}}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DecOIErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathrm{PCalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}).

Proof.

In the distributional setting, we rewrite the definition of Decision OI.
We write the difference of losses in terms of the difference of their discrete derivatives,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{all}}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DecOIErr}_{\mathcal{D}}(p)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{all}}}\big|\E_{x,y\sim\mathcal{D}}[\ell(k_{\ell}\circ p(x),y)]-\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim\mathcal{D}\\
\tilde{y}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p(x))\end{subarray}}[\ell(k_{\ell}\circ p(x),\tilde{y})]\big|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{all}}}\big|\E_{x,y\sim\mathcal{D}}[y\cdot\Delta\ell(k_{\ell}\circ p(x))-p(x)\cdot\Delta\ell(k_{\ell}\circ p(x))]\big|

where the equality follows by the fact that, in the modeled world,
\E[\tilde{y}|x]=p(x)
and the definition of
\Delta\ell
.
Then, we can combine terms and consider the supremum.

\displaystyle\sup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{all}}}\big|\E_{x,y\sim\mathcal{D}}[\Delta\ell(k_{\ell}\circ p(x))\cdot(y-p(x))]\big|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}}\big|\E_{x,y\sim\mathcal{D}}[\Delta\ell(p(x))\cdot(y-p(x))]\big|

The final equality, here, follows from the fact that for any loss,
\ell
composed with the optimal post-processing
k_{\ell}
, can be viewed as a proper loss.
Formally, for any
\ell:[0,1]\to[-1,1]
, there exists a proper loss
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}
(namely,
\ell^{\prime}(p,y)=\ell(k_{\ell}(p),y)
) such that
\Delta\ell(k_{\ell}(p))=\Delta\ell^{\prime}(p)
, for all
p\in[0,1]
.

Note that the same argument works in the sequential setting, establishing the analogous equality.
∎

Using this view on Proper Calibration, we can immediately apply the Loss OI framework to achieve omnipredictors, with proper calibration replacing Decision OI / calibration.

Lemma 3.2
.

For a sequence of
T
predictions
\mathbf{p}
and context, outcome pairs
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
,

(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{OmniRegret}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\mathrm{PCalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+(\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

The proof of lemma mirrors that of Proposition 4.5 in
Gopalan et al., (2023)
in that it explicitly follows the Loss OI framework introduced in the paper.
For completeness and self-containment, we give a proof of the statement in the sequential setting.

Proof of
Lemma

3.2
.

Recall that

(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{OmniRegret}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\max_{\{h\in\mathcal{H},\ell\in\mathcal{L}\}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})),y_{t})-\ell(h(x_{t}),y_{t})

For a fixed timestep
t\in[T]
, we expand the inner expression as follows:

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})),y_{t})-\ell(h(x_{t}),y_{t})

(12)

\displaystyle\leq\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})),y_{t})+\E_{\tilde{y}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p_{t}(x_{t}))}[\ell(h(x_{t}),\tilde{y})]-\E_{\tilde{y}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p_{t}(x_{t}))}[\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})),\tilde{y})]-\ell(h(x_{t}),y_{t})

(13)

\displaystyle=\left[\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})),y_{t})-\E_{\tilde{y}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p_{t}(x_{t}))}[\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})),\tilde{y})]\right]+\left[\E_{\tilde{y}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p_{t}(x_{t}))}[\ell(h(x_{t}),\tilde{y})]-\ell(h(x_{t}),y_{t})\right]

(14)

where the second line follows from the definition of
k_{\ell}
. Now we simplify both terms separately. For the first term, we have

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})),y_{t})-\E_{\tilde{y}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p_{t}(x_{t}))}[\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})),\tilde{y})]

(15)

\displaystyle=[y_{t}\Delta\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})))+\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})),0)]-[p_{t}\Delta\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})))+\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t}))),0)]

(16)

\displaystyle=(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\Delta\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})))

(17)

where line (6) uses the fact that
\ell(p,y)=y(\ell(p,1)-\ell(p,0))+\ell(p,0)
for
y\in\{0,1\}

Similarly, for the second term, we have

\displaystyle\E_{\tilde{y}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p_{t}(x_{t}))}

\displaystyle[\ell(h(x_{t}),\tilde{y})]-\ell(h(x_{t}),y_{t})

(18)

\displaystyle=[p_{t}(x_{t})\Delta\ell(h(x_{t}))+\ell(h(x_{t}),0)]-[y_{t}\Delta\ell(h(x_{t}))+\ell(h(x_{t}),0)]

(19)

\displaystyle=(p_{t}(x_{t})-y_{t})\Delta\ell(h(x_{t}))

(20)

Plugging these simplifications back into the regret term, we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{OmniRegret}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\{h\in\mathcal{H},\ell\in\mathcal{L}\}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\Delta\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})))+(p_{t}(x_{t})-y_{t})\Delta\ell(h(x_{t}))

(21)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}\}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\Delta\ell(k_{\ell}(p_{t}(x_{t})))\right|+\max_{\{h\in\mathcal{H},\ell\in\mathcal{L}\}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}(p_{t}(x_{t})-y_{t})\Delta\ell(h(x_{t}))\right|

(22)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}\}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\Delta\ell(p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|+\max_{\{h\in\mathcal{H},\ell\in\mathcal{L}\}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}(p_{t}(x_{t})-y_{t})\Delta\ell(h(x_{t}))\right|

(23)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{PCalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+(\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

(24)

where line (
23
) follows from the fact that
\Delta\ell
composed with its
k_{\ell}
function corresponds to some
\Delta\ell_{\mathrm{proper}}
for some proper loss
\ell_{\mathrm{proper}}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}
.
∎

Naturally, proper calibration can be incorporated into the original algorithms for learning omnipredictors via Loss OI.
In
Appendix

C
, we describe an adaptation of the boosting-style algorithm of
Gopalan et al., (2023)
that outputs an omnipredictor using a multiplicative factor of
1/\varepsilon^{6}
fewer samples than the original algorithm based on
\ell_{1}
-calibration.

3.2
Approximating Proper Calibration with Weighted Calibration over Thresholds

To achieve calibration for all proper weight functions, we will take advantage of the basis decomposition of proper scoring losses in
(
Li et al.,, 2022
;
Kleinberg et al.,, 2023
)
. To this end, we introduce a notion of calibration, weighted by threshold functions that utilizes the basis functions as weight functions to approximate proper calibration effectively.
Concretely, we consider the following characterization of proper losses.

Lemma 3.3

(V-shaped proper losses
(
Li et al.,, 2022
;
Kleinberg et al.,, 2023
)
)
.

For
v\in[0,1]
, define the proper loss
\ell_{v}(p,y)=(y-v)\text{sgn}(v-p)
.
Then every
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}
can be expressed as a convex combination of these
v
-shaped proper losses. That is, for every
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}
, there exists nonnegative coefficients
c_{v}(\ell)
such that
\int_{0}^{1}c_{v}(\ell)dv\leq 2
and

\ell(p,y)=\int_{0}^{1}c_{v}(\ell)\ell_{v}(p,y)dv

Immediately, this characterization also gives a characterization of the discrete derivatives of proper losses.

Corollary 3.4
.

For every
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}
, there exists nonnegative coefficients
c_{v}(\ell)
such that
\int_{0}^{1}c_{v}(\ell)dv\leq 2
and

\Delta\ell(p)=\int_{0}^{1}c_{v}(\ell)\Delta\ell_{v}(p)dv

where
\Delta\ell_{v}(p)=\text{sgn}(v-p)
is a {-1,1} threshold function at
v
.

In the
Lemma

3.5
, we show that proper calibration and calibration weighted by threshold functions are within a constant factor of each other.
Specifically, we let
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}
denote the set of weight functions associated with
V
-shaped proper scoring rules, where each weight function
w
corresponds to the discrete derivative of a
V
-shaped proper loss function i.e
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}=\{\text{sgn}(v-p):v\in[0,1]\}
.
Then, we obtain the following tight approximation of the proper calibration error.

Lemma 3.5
.

For a sequence of
T
predictions
\mathbf{p}
and context, outcome pairs
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
,

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\mathrm{PCalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq 2\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

Proof.

We wish to show that

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{proper}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq 2\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

By
Corollary

3.4
, we know that for every
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}
, there exists nonnegative coefficients
c_{v}(\ell)
such that
\int_{0}^{1}c_{v}(\ell)dv\leq 2

\Delta\ell(p)=\int_{0}^{1}c_{v}(\ell)\Delta\ell_{v}(p)dv

\displaystyle\max_{w\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{proper}}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}w(p_{t}(x_{t}))(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|

\displaystyle=\max_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Delta\ell(p_{t}(x_{t}))(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|

(25)

\displaystyle=\max_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\int_{0}^{1}c_{v}(\ell)\Delta\ell_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))dv\right)(y_{t}-p_{t}(x))\right|

(
Corollary

3.4
)

\displaystyle=\max_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}}\left|\int_{0}^{1}c_{v}(\ell)\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Delta\ell_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))(y_{t}-p_{t}(x))\right)dv\right|

(26)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}}\left|\int_{0}^{1}c_{v}(\ell)\sup_{v\in[0,1]}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Delta\ell_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))(y_{t}-p_{t}(x))\right)dv\right|

(27)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}}\left|\int_{0}^{1}c_{v}(\ell)dv\right|\sup_{v\in[0,1]}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Delta\ell_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))(y_{t}-p_{t}(x))\right|

(28)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{v\in[0,1]}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Delta\ell_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))(y_{t}-p_{t}(x))\right|

(29)

Thus,

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{proper}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq 2\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

The first inequality
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{proper}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}\subset\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{proper}}

∎

Lemma

3.5
bounds the proper calibration in terms of a notion of weighted calibration with an uncountably infinite collection of weight functions
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}
.
Towards a practically-realizable algorithm for proper calibration, we show that for sufficiently discretized predictions, weighted calibration with respect to a discretized collection of thresholds suffices to bound the proper calibration error.
Let
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}^{\gamma}
denote a
\gamma
-discretization of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}
. That is,
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}^{\gamma}=\{\text{sgn}(v-p):v\in\{0,\gamma,2\gamma,\ldots,1\}\}
.

Lemma 3.6

(
\gamma
-discretized thresholds)
.

For a sequence of
T
predictions
\mathbf{p}
with values in
\{0,\gamma,2\gamma,\ldots,1\}
and context, outcome pairs
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
,

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}^{\gamma}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

Proof of
Lemma

3.6
.

Recall that

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\max_{v\in[0,1]}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\text{sgn}(v-p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|.

Since the predictions
p_{t}(x_{t})
take values only in
\{0,\gamma,\ldots,1\}
, the sign function
\text{sgn}(v-p_{t}(x_{t}))
can only change at points in
\{0,\gamma,\ldots,1\}
. Hence, for any
v\in[0,1]
, there exists some
v^{\prime}\in\{0,\gamma,\ldots,1\}
such that:

\text{sgn}(v-p_{t}(x_{t}))=\text{sgn}(v^{\prime}-p_{t}(x_{t})).

This implies

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\text{sgn}(v-p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\text{sgn}(v^{\prime}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t})).

Therefore

\max_{v\in[0,1]}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\text{sgn}(v-p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|=\max_{v^{\prime}\in\{0,\gamma,\ldots,1\}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\text{sgn}(v^{\prime}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|.

Thus

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}^{\gamma}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}).

∎

3.3
Algorithm for Proper Calibration in the Online Setting

In this subsection, we present an algorithm that guarantees proper calibration in the online setting at the rate of
O(\sqrt{T\log T})
.

Algorithm

1
achieves this by ensuring proper calibration by auditing with threshold weight functions per
Lemma

3.5
.
Moreover, the rate of our algorithm is nearly tight; no algorithm can guarantee expected threshold calibration error at a rate of
\Omega(\sqrt{T})
.
This lower bound is unsurprising, but follows by
Section

A.2
, where we show that U-calibration lower bounds a multiplicative factor of proper calibration.

Overview of
Algorithm

1
:

The algorithm is based on Blackwell’s Approachability Theorem. We define a two player game where the adversary player selects
z_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
and the learner selects
p_{t}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[1/T]
. Both players are allowed to play randomized strategies but since the learner observes
x_{t}
, we can simplify things and only consider
y_{t}\in\{0,1\}
and
p_{t}=p_{t}(x_{t})
. We design the payoff vector of this game to reflect our objective of threshold calibration. That is, define

u_{v,s}(p_{t},y_{t})=s(y_{t}-p_{t})\mathrm{sgn}(v-p_{t})\quad\text{for}\ v\in[1/T],s\in\{+,-\}

Observe that after T rounds of interaction,

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}\text{-}\mathrm{CalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\max_{v,s}\sum_{t\in[T]}u_{v,s}(p_{t},z_{t})
. Therefore, we design the learner’s target set to be the set of all vectors
u
with coordinates less than
1/T
.

We use exponential weights update method in
2
to generate sequence of halfspaces
w^{t}
with coordinates for every
v\in[1/T],s\in\{+,-\}
. Given a halfspace
w^{t}
, the algorithm computes the function
f(q)
defined in
3
and the algorithm chooses the distribution to sample
p_{t}
using the strategy described in
4
to
10
.

In all, we establish the following regret bound.

Theorem 3.7

(Proper Calibration Upper Bound)
.

Algorithm

1
guarantees expected proper calibration error of
O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}\right)

Formally, we leverage the following lemmas that give guarantees on the Blackwell optimal response (in
4
to
10
) and the dual player’s strategy (in
2
). This dual player strategy bears similarity in structure to the algorithms used for classical calibration
(
Foster and Vohra,, 1998
;
Gupta et al.,, 2022
;
Okoroafor et al.,, 2024
)
.

Algorithm 1

Proper Calibration

Input:
Sequence of samples
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{T}\}

Output:
Sequence of (randomized) predictors
p_{1},\ldots,p_{T}

1:

for
each
t\in[T]

do

2:

Let
w_{v,s}^{t}:=\frac{\exp\left(\eta\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}u_{v,s}(p_{i},z_{i})\right)}{\sum_{v^{\prime},s^{\prime}}\exp\left(\eta\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}u_{v^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(p_{i},z_{i})\right)}
for all
v\in[1/T],s\in\{+,-\}

3:

Compute

f(q)=\sum_{v,s}w^{t}_{v,s}\cdot s\cdot\mathrm{sgn}(v-q)

4:

if

f(0)\leq 0

then

5:

Predict
p_{t}=0

6:

else

if

f(1)>0

then

7:

Predict
p_{t}=1

8:

else

9:

Find adjacent probabilities
q_{i},q_{i+1}
such that
f(q_{i})\cdot f(q_{i+1})\leq 0

10:

Predict
p_{t}=q_{i}
with prob
\frac{|f(q_{i+1})|}{|f(q_{i})|+|f(q_{i+1})|}
and
p_{t}=q_{i+1}
with prob
\frac{|f(q_{i})|}{|f(q_{i})|+|f(q_{i+1})|}

11:

Observe
x_{t}
, predict
p_{t}
, and then observe
y_{t}

Lemma 3.8

(Halfspace Approachability)
.

Given a halfspace
w
, the strategy described in
4
to
10
outputs a distribution
p_{t}
over
[1/T]
such that
\E_{p_{t}}[\langle w,u(p_{t},z_{t})\rangle]\leq 1/T
for any choice of
z_{t}

Proof.

We consider the cases in the strategy separately:

Case 1:
If
f(0)\leq 0
, predict
p_{t}=0
.
Then for
v\in[1/T],s\in\{+,-\}
,

w_{v,s}u_{v,s}(p_{t},z_{t})=s(y_{t}-p_{t})\mathrm{sgn}(v-p_{t})w_{v,s}=sy_{t}w_{v,s}

Summing over values of
v,s
and applying the definition of
f
in
3
, we get

\langle w,u(p_{t},z_{t})\rangle=y_{t}f(0)\leq 0\quad\text{for any choice of}\ y\in\{0,1\}

Case 2:
If
f(1)>0
, predict
p_{t}=1
.
Then for
v\in[1/T],s\in\{+,-\}
,

w_{v,s}u_{v,s}(p_{t},z_{t})=s(y_{t}-p_{t})\mathrm{sgn}(v-p_{t})w_{v,s}=sy_{t}w_{v,s}

Summing over values of
v,s
and applying the definition of
f
in
3
, we get

\langle w,u(p_{t},z_{t})\rangle=(y_{t}-1)f(1)\leq 0\quad\text{for any choice of}\ y\in\{0,1\}

Case 3:
Find adjacent probabilities
q_{i},q_{i+1}
such that
f(q_{i})\cdot f(q_{i+1})\leq 0
.

We wish to bound
\E_{p_{t}}[\langle w,u(p_{t},y)\rangle]
for both possible outcomes
y\in\{0,1\}
.
Let’s fix
v,s
and compute
\E_{\mathbf{p}}[w_{v,s}u_{v,s}]

\frac{|f(q_{i+1})|}{|f(q_{i})|+|f(q_{i+1})|}\left[s(y-q_{i})\mathrm{sgn}(v-q_{i})w_{v,s}\right]+\frac{|f(q_{i})|}{|f(q_{i})|+|f(q_{i+1})|}\left[s(y-q_{i+1})\mathrm{sgn}(v-q_{i+1})w_{v,s}\right]

Summing over all pairs of
(v,s)
and applying the definition of
f
in
3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{|f(q_{i+1})|}{|f(q_{i})|+|f(q_{i+1})|}\left[(y-q_{i})f(q_{i})\right]+\frac{|f(q_{i})|}{|f(q_{i})|+|f(q_{i+1})|}\left[(y-q_{i+1})f(q_{i+1})\right]

(30)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|f(q_{i})||f(q_{i+1})|}{|f(q_{i})|+|f(q_{i+1})|}(q_{i+1}-q_{i})

(31)

\displaystyle\leq 1/T

(since
f(q)\in[-1,1]
)

∎

Lemma 3.9

(Exponential Weight Updates
Arora et al., (2012)
)
.

The exponential weight updates in
2
provide a sequence of vectors
w^{t}
such that

\max_{w:||w||_{1}=1}\left\langle w,\sum_{t\in[T]}u(p_{t},z_{t})\right\rangle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle w^{t},u(p_{t},z_{t})\rangle+O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}\right)

With these lemmas in place, we can prove
Theorem

3.7
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.7
.

We wish to bound the expected threshold calibration error, that is,
\E_{\mathbf{p}}\left[\max_{v,s}\sum_{t\in[T]}u_{v,s}(p_{t},z_{t})\right]
where the expectation is over the randomness in the sampling of
p_{t}
. Note that this is the same as

\E_{\mathbf{p}}\left[\max_{w:||w||_{1}=1}\left\langle w,\sum_{t\in[T]}u(p_{t},z_{t})\right\rangle\right]
.

\displaystyle\E_{\mathbf{p}}\left[\max_{w:||w||_{1}=1}\left\langle w,\sum_{t\in[T]}u(p_{t},z_{t})\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\leq\E_{\mathbf{p}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle w^{t},u(p_{t},z_{t})\rangle\right]+O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}\right)

(by
Lemma

3.9
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\E_{\mathbf{p}}\langle w^{t},u(p_{t},z_{t})\rangle+O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}\right)

(by linearity of expectation)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\E_{w^{t}}\left[\left.\E_{p_{t}}\langle w^{t},u(p_{t},z_{t})\rangle\right|w^{t}\right]+O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}\right)

(by law of iterated expectations)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tfrac{1}{T}\right)+O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}\right)

(by
Lemma

3.8
)

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}\right)

∎

In all, proper calibration is an intermediate notion: strong enough to imply omniprediction via the Loss OI framework, but weak enough that it can be achieved asymptotically efficiently compared to
\ell_{1}
-calibration.

3.4
Augmented Proper Calibration

To aid with our goal of achieving proper calibration and multiaccuracy simultaneously, we introduce the following augmented proper calibration problem.
We are given a sequence of samples
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{T},y_{T})
and hypothesis functions

q_{1},\ldots,q_{T}\,:\,\mathcal{X}\to[-1,1]
and the goal is to output a sequence of predictors
p_{1},\ldots,p_{T}\,:\,\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
that is proper calibrated, i.e.
\mathrm{PCalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is sublinear in
T
, while also satisfying the sequential multiaccuracy guarantee
\sum_{t=1}^{T}q_{t}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\in o(T)
with respect to the sequence of input hypothesis functions
q_{1},\ldots,q_{T}\,:\,\mathcal{X}\to[-1,1]
. To solve this problem we present the algorithm, Augmented Proper Calibration (
apcal
), and state its performance guarantees in both the sequential and distributional settings.

Proposition 3.10

(Augmented Proper Calibrator)
.

Given an online sequence of samples
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{T},y_{T})
and hypothesis functions
q_{1},\ldots,q_{T}
,

Algorithm

2

outputs a sequence of predictors
p_{1},\ldots,p_{T}
such that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

1.

\mathrm{PCalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T/\delta}\right)

2.

\sum_{t=1}^{T}q_{t}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T/\delta}\right)
.

If the samples
\{(x_{t},y_{t})\}_{t=1}^{T}
are drawn i.i.d. from a distribution
\mathcal{D}

(while the functions
q_{1},\ldots,q_{T}
may still be specified online by an adaptive adversary)
then the uniform distribution
\hat{\mathbf{p}}=\mathrm{unif}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{T})
satisfies the
following bounds with probability at least
1-\delta
.

1.

\mathrm{PCalErr}_{\mathcal{D}}(\hat{\mathbf{p}})\leq O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\ln(T/\delta)}{T}}\right)

2.

\tfrac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}[q_{t}(x)(y-p_{t}(x))]\leq O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\ln(T/\delta)}{T}}\right)
.

The algorithm runs in
\mathrm{poly}(T)
timesteps per iteration and each
p_{t}
is simply a monotonic remapping of
q_{t}

to
[1/T]
. In other words, there exists a monotone non-decreasing function
{\Upsilon}_{t}:[-1,1]\to[1/T]
, randomly sampled by the algorithm, such that
p_{t}(x)={\Upsilon}_{t}(q_{t}(x))
for all
x
.

Algorithm 2

Augmented Proper Calibration

1:

procedure

MinZeroCrossing
(
g,q
)

\triangleright
Find lexicographically minimal
(j,\lambda)
such that
g(q,j,\lambda)\leq 0

2:

if

g(q,0,0)\leq 0

then

3:

return
(0,0)

4:

else

5:

for

j=0,\frac{1}{T},\frac{2}{T},\ldots,\frac{T-1}{T}

do

6:

if

g(q,j,0)\cdot g(q,j,1)\leq 0

then

\triangleright
Solve
(1-\lambda)g(q,j,0)+\lambda g(q,j,1)=0
for
\lambda

7:

Let
\lambda=\frac{|g(q,j,0)|}{|g(q,j,0)|+|g(q,j,1)|}

8:

return

(j,\lambda)

9:

return
(1,1)

10:

11:

procedure

APCAL
(
T
)

12:

Input:
Sequence of samples
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{T},y_{T})

13:

Input:
Sequence of hypothesis functions
q_{1},\ldots,q_{T}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[-1,1]

14:

Output:
Sequence of predictors
p_{1},\ldots,p_{T}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[0,1]

15:

Initialize weights
w_{v,s}^{1}=\tfrac{1}{2T+3}
for
v\in[1/T],s\in\{+,-\}

\triangleright
for proper calibration constraints

16:

Initialize weight:
w^{1}_{\mathrm{sma}}=\tfrac{1}{2T+3}

\triangleright
for sequential multiaccuracy constraint

17:

Set
\eta=\sqrt{\log(T)/T}

\triangleright
learning rate for exponential weight updates

18:

for

t=1
to
T

do

19:

Sample
\zeta\in[0,1]
uniformly at random.

20:

Define functions

f_{t},\,g_{t},\,\psi_{t},{\Upsilon}_{t}
as follows.

\displaystyle f_{t}(q,j)

\displaystyle=w^{t}_{\mathrm{sma}}\cdot q+\sum_{v,s}w_{v,s}^{t}\cdot\mathrm{Th}_{v}(j)

(32)

\displaystyle g_{t}(q,j,\lambda)

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)f_{t}(q,j)\,+\,\lambda f_{t}\left(q,\min\{j+\tfrac{1}{T},1\}\right)

(33)

\displaystyle\psi_{t}(q)

\displaystyle=\mbox{\sc MinZeroCrossing}(g_{t},q)

(34)

\displaystyle{\Upsilon}_{t}(q)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}j&\mbox{if }\psi_{t}(q)=(j,\lambda)\mbox{ where }\lambda>\zeta\\
j+\tfrac{1}{T}&\mbox{if }\psi_{t}(q)=(j,\lambda)\mbox{ where }\lambda\leq\zeta\end{cases}

(35)

21:

Receive

q_{t}

22:

Return
predictor
p_{t}={\Upsilon}_{t}\circ q_{t}

23:

Observe

(x_{t},y_{t})

24:

Perform exponential weight updates with losses
s\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot\mathrm{Th}_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))
for each
v,s
and

(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot q_{t}(x_{t})
for the sequential multiaccuracy constraint:

\displaystyle W^{t}

\displaystyle=e^{-\eta\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot q_{t}(x_{t})}w^{t}_{\mathrm{sma}}+\sum_{v,s}e^{-\eta\cdot s\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot\mathrm{Th}_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))}w_{v,s}^{t}

\displaystyle w^{t+1}_{\mathrm{sma}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\eta\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot q_{t}(x_{t})}w^{t}_{\mathrm{sma}}\,\big/\,W^{t}

\displaystyle w^{t+1}_{v,s}

\displaystyle=e^{-\eta\cdot s\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot\mathrm{Th}_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))}w_{v,s}^{t}\,\big/\,W^{t}

Overview of
Algorithm

2
:

The algorithm follows the design ideas of
Algorithm

1
. We consider a two player game that combines the objectives of proper calibration and sequential multiaccuracy. The adversary selects
z_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
and the learner selects
p_{t}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[1/T]
. We construct a payoff vector where the first
2T+2
components are for the proper calibration objective

u_{v,s}(p_{t},z_{t})=s(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\mathrm{Th}_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))\quad\text{for}\ v\in[1/T],s\in\{+,-\}

and the last component captures the sequential multiaccuracy constraint from the sequence of hypothesis functions
q_{t}

u_{\mathrm{ma}}(p_{t},z_{t})=(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))q_{t}(x_{t})

The learner’s target set is the set of vectors
u
whose coordinates are bounded by 0 i.e the negative orthant. We will maintain weights for these
2T+3
dimensions of the payoff vector using exponential weights.

Concretely, in
Algorithm

2
, we use exponential weights update to generate sequence of halfspaces
w^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2T+3}
with coordinates for every pair
v\in[1/T],s\in\{+,-\}
and the sequential multiaccuracy constraint. Then for each timestep
t
, the algorithm constructs the predictor based on the strategy described from
20
to
22
.

The following lemmas are used in the proof of the proposition.

Lemma 3.11

(Halfspace Approachability)
.

For every timestep
t\in[T]
, the predictor
p_{t}(x)
constructed from input
q_{t}
in line
22
satisfies the following guarantee for all
x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\{0,1\}
:

\E_{p_{t}}\left[w^{t}_{\mathrm{sma}}\cdot q_{t}(x)(y-p_{t}(x))+\sum_{v,s}w_{v,s}^{t}\cdot s\cdot\mathrm{Th}_{v}(p_{t}(x))\cdot(y-p_{t}(x))\right]\leq\frac{1}{T}

Proof of
Lemma

3.11
.

Recall the definition of
f_{t}(q,j)
in
Equation

32
, and recall that
p_{t}(x)={\Upsilon}_{t}(q_{t}(x))
.
We can rewrite the objective above as showing that for all
x,y
, and
q=q_{t}(x)
,

\E_{j\sim{\Upsilon}_{t}(q)}\left[f_{t}(q,j)\cdot(y-j)\right]\leq\tfrac{1}{T}

We now consider three cases:

•

Case 1: If
f_{t}(q,0)\leq 0
, then
g_{t}(q,0,0)\leq 0
so
\psi_{t}(q)=(0,0)
and

{\Upsilon}_{t}(q)=0
as long as
\zeta>0
(which happens almost surely).

\displaystyle\E_{j\sim{\Upsilon}_{t}(q)}\left[f_{t}(q,j)\cdot(y-j)\right]

\displaystyle=f_{t}(q,0)y

\displaystyle\leq\max\{0,f_{t}(q,0)\}

(for any value of
y\in\{0,1\}
)

\displaystyle\leq 0

(by starting assumption
f_{t}(q,0)\leq 0
)

•

Case 2: If
f_{t}(q,j)>0
for all
j\in[1/T]
, then
g_{t}(q,j,\lambda)>0
for all

(j,\lambda)\in[1/T]\times[0,1]
, so
\psi_{t}(q)=(1,1)
and
{\Upsilon}_{t}(q)=1
for any value of
\zeta
.

\displaystyle\E_{j\sim{\Upsilon}_{t}(q)}\left[f_{t}(q,j)\cdot(y-j)\right]

\displaystyle=f_{t}(q,1)(y-1)

\displaystyle\leq\max\{0,-f_{t}(q,1)\}

(for any value of
y\in\{0,1\}
)

\displaystyle\leq 0

(by starting assumption
f_{t}(q,1)>0
)

•

Case 3: There exist adjacent probabilities
i,i+\frac{1}{T}
in
[1/T]
such that

f_{t}\left(q,i\right)\cdot f_{t}\left(q,i+\tfrac{1}{T}\right)\leq 0.

(36)

Then, for the minimum
i\in[1/T]
satisfying (
36
), the values

g_{t}(q,i,0)
and
g_{t}(q,i,1)
are differently signed, so there is a unique
\lambda\in[0,1]

satisfying
g_{t}(q,i,\lambda)=0
, and
\psi_{t}(q)=(i,\lambda)
.

\displaystyle\E_{j\sim{\Upsilon}_{t}(q)}\left[f_{t}(q,j)\cdot(y-j)\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr(\zeta>\lambda)\cdot f_{t}(q,i)(y-i)+\Pr(\zeta\leq\lambda)\cdot f_{t}(q,i+\tfrac{1}{T})(y-i-\tfrac{1}{T})

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda)f_{t}(q,i)(y-i)+\lambda f_{t}(q,i+\tfrac{1}{T})(y-i-\tfrac{1}{T})

\displaystyle=g_{t}(q,i,\lambda)(y-i)-\frac{\lambda f_{t}(q,i+\tfrac{1}{T})}{T}

\displaystyle\leq 1/T

(since
g_{t}(q,i,\lambda)=0,\lambda\in[0,1]
, and
|f_{t}(q,i+\frac{1}{T})|\leq 1
.)

∎

Lemma 3.12

(Halfspace Concentration)
.

The following inequality holds with probability at least
1-\delta/2
for the sequence of inputs
(x_{1},y_{1},q_{1}),\ldots,(x_{T},y_{T},q_{T})
, weights
w^{1},\ldots,w^{T}
and outputs
p_{1},\ldots,p_{T}
:

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[w^{t}_{\mathrm{sma}}\cdot q_{t}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))+\sum_{v,s}w_{v,s}^{t}\cdot s\cdot\mathrm{Th}_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right]\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln 1/\delta}\right)

Proof of
Lemma

3.12
.

The conclusion of the lemma can be rewritten as showing that

\sum_{t=1}^{T}f(x_{t},p_{t}(x_{t}))(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln 1/\delta}\right)

We showed in
Lemma

3.11
that
\E_{j\sim p_{t}(x)}\left[f(x,j)\cdot(y-j)\right]\leq\tfrac{1}{T}
. Now we apply Azuma-Hoeffding by defining the martingale difference sequence

Z_{t}=f(x_{t},p_{t}(x_{t}))(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))-\E_{p_{t}(x_{t})}\left[f(x_{t},p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right]
, where the expectation is over the randomness of the sampling of
p_{t}
. Note that
Z_{t}
is bounded by
2
. Applying Azuma-Hoeffding, we have that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln 1/\delta}\right)

Since
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\E_{p_{t}(x_{t})}\left[f(x_{t},p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right]\leq T\cdot 1/T=1
, we obtain the desired result.
∎

We conclude this subsection with the proof of
Proposition

3.10
.

Proof of
Proposition

3.10
.

First, we upper bound the expected error, in the sequential setting, of the predictors the algorithm outputs.
Let
u(p_{t},z_{t})
denote the
2T+3
dimensional payoff vector where the first
2T+2
coordinates correspond to the proper calibration constraints and the
2T+3
-th coordinate corresponds to the sequential multiaccuracy constraint. The algorithm maintains a sequence of weight vectors
w^{t}
over these
2T+3
constraints that ensures the following inequalities are satisfied by any weight vector
w\succeq\mathbf{0}
with
\|w\|_{1}=1
.

\displaystyle\left\langle w,\sum_{t\in[T]}u(p_{t},z_{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle w^{t},u(p_{t},z_{t})\rangle+O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}\right)

(by exponential weights guarantee)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[w^{t}_{\mathrm{sma}}\cdot q_{t}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))+\sum_{v,s}w_{v,s}^{t}\cdot s\cdot\mathrm{Th}_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))\cdot(y-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right]+O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}\right)

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln 1/\delta}\right)+O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}\right)

(by
Lemma

3.12
)

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T/\delta}\right)

Instantiating the inequality while setting
w
equal to the first
2T+2
standard basis vectors, we obtain an upper bound on the
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}^{1/T}
-weighted calibration error of the sequence

\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
. Observing that each predictor
p_{t}
takes values in the set
[1/T]=\{0,\frac{1}{T},\frac{2}{T},\ldots,1\}

and that the proper calibration error of a
[1/T]
-valued
predictor is bounded by twice its
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Th}}^{1/T}
-weighted calibration error
Lemma

3.6
, we obtain the stated upper bound on
\mathrm{PCalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Instantiating the inequality while setting

w
equal to the last standard basis vector, we obtain the upper bound on
\sum_{t=1}^{T}q_{t}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))
.

To bound the expected error of
\hat{\mathbf{p}}=\mathrm{unif}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{T})
in the distributional setting we again use a martingale argument.
Define

Z_{t}(v,s)=\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}[s\cdot\mathrm{Th}_{v}(p_{t}(x))(y-p_{t}(x))]-s\cdot\mathrm{Th}_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))

for
v\in[1/T],s\in\{+,-\}
. Observe that
Z_{t}(v,s)
for
t=1,\ldots,T
forms a martingale difference sequence with bounded variance of 2.
Similarly, let

Z_{t}^{\mathrm{ma}}=\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}[q_{t}(x)(y-p_{t}(x))]-q_{t}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t})).

Z_{t}^{\mathrm{ma}}
for
t=1,\ldots,T
forms a martingale difference sequence with bounded variance of 2.
Thus, applying Azuma-Hoeffding’s inequality together with union bound over values of
v\in[1/T],s\in\{+,-\}
and
Z_{t}^{\mathrm{ma}}
, we get that, with probability at least
1-\delta
, for all
v\in[1/T],s\in\{+,-\}

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}(v,s)\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{\ln\tfrac{2T}{\delta}}{T}}

\displaystyle\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\left|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}^{\mathrm{ma}}\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{\ln\tfrac{2T}{\delta}}{T}}

The distributional error bounds now follow by observing that

\displaystyle\forall v,s\;\;\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}[s\cdot\mathrm{Th}_{v}(p_{t}(x))(y-p_{t}(x))]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}s\cdot\mathrm{Th}_{v}(p_{t}(x_{t}))(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\,+\,\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}(v,s)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}[q_{t}(x)(y-p_{t}(x)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}q_{t}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\,+\,\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}^{\mathrm{ma}}

and that we have already shown all terms on the right side of both inequalities are bounded
above by
O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log T/\delta}{T}}\right).

The algorithm runs in
\mathrm{poly}(T)
timesteps per iteration and each
p_{t}
is defined in line
22
as a composition

{\Upsilon}_{t}\circ q_{t}
where
{\Upsilon}_{t}:[-1,1]\to[1/T]
. To see that

{\Upsilon}_{t}(q)
is a monotone function of
q
, observe in equation (
32
) that
for each
j\in[1/T]
,
f_{t}(q,j)
is a monotone increasing function of
q
because
w^{t}_{\mathrm{sma}}>0
.
Hence, for each
(j,\lambda)
, the function
g_{t}(q,j,\lambda)
is a monotone increasing function of
q
.
It follows that
\psi_{t}(q)
is a monotone (lexicographically) non-decreasing function of
q
: if

\psi_{t}(q)=(j,\lambda)
and
q^{\prime}<q
, then
g_{t}(q^{\prime},j,\lambda)<g_{t}(q,j,\lambda)\leq 0
, so

\psi_{t}(q^{\prime})
cannot be lexicographically greater than
(j,\lambda)
.
For any fixed
\zeta
, the lexicographic monotonicity of
\psi_{t}
implies the
monotonicity of
{\Upsilon}_{t}
.
∎

4
Online Omniprediction

In this section, we show that online omniprediction for a class of hypothesis functions
\mathcal{H}
and a class of loss functions
\mathcal{L}
can be achieved using an online weak agnostic learner for
\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
with regret that scales with
\tilde{O}\left(\sqrt{T}\right)
and the regret of the online weak agnostic learner.
Online weak agnostic learning, as studied in
(
Chen et al.,, 2012
;
Brukhim et al.,, 2020
;
Beygelzimer et al.,, 2015
)
, outputs a sequence of predictions whose correlation with the outcome sequence is not much less than that of the best fixed hypothesis in hindsight.

Definition 4.1

(Online Weak Agnostic Learner)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\{c:\mathcal{X}\to[-1,1]\}
be a hypothesis class, and fix a failure probability
\delta\in(0,1)
. An
online weak agnostic learner
interacting over
T
rounds computes a sequence of randomized strategies
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{T}\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}\to[-1,1])
, where each
Q_{t}
is a distribution over predictors
\mathcal{X}\to[-1,1]
. When called as a subroutine (“oracle”) within another algorithm, the learner outputs random samples from the distribution
Q_{t}
corresponding to the current interaction round.

The learner interacts with an adaptive adversary under the
simultaneous-move
(SM) protocol: at each round
t
, the learner selects
Q_{t}\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[-1,1])
, and the adversary simultaneously selects a context-label pair
(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathcal{X}\times[-1,1]
, potentially as a function of past interactions but without access to the specific realization
q_{t}\sim Q_{t}
.

With probability at least
1-\delta
over the learner’s internal randomness, the learner guarantees:

\max_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}c(x_{t})y_{t}\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\E_{q_{t}\sim Q_{t}}[q_{t}(x_{t})y_{t}]+\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T),

where
\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T)
is the regret bound of the learner with respect to the class
\mathcal{C}
.

Remark 4.1
.

In our omniprediction setting, the learner will be used in a
delayed-label
(DL) setting, where the label
y_{t}
at round
t
may depend on one or more realizations
q_{t}^{1},\ldots,q_{t}^{k}\sim Q_{t}
but the context
x_{t}
may only depend on realizations
q_{s}^{j}\;(s<t)
drawn from
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{t-1}
in previous interaction rounds. In such cases, one may convert the SM-regret guarantee into a high-probability DL guarantee via implicit resampling; see
Lemma

4.7
.

Using an online weak agnostic learner for
\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
, we present the following algorithm for omniprediction.

Algorithm 3

Online Omniprediction

1:

procedure

OnlineOmniprediction
(
T
)

2:

Input:
Sequence of samples
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{T},y_{T})

3:

Input:
A failure probability parameter,
\delta>0
.

4:

Input:
An online weak agnostic learner,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{owal}}
, for function class
C=\{-1,+1\}\cdot\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
with
k
-fold resampling (
Lemma

4.7
) for
k=O(T\log(T/\delta))
and failure probability
\delta/2

5:

Input:
An augmented proper calibrator (
Algorithm

2
),
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{apcal}}
, with failure probability
\delta/2

6:

Output:
Sequence of predictors
p_{1},\ldots,p_{T}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[0,1]

7:

Receive

q_{1}
from
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{owal}}

8:

for

t=1
to
T

do

9:

Send

q_{t}
and sample
(x_{t},y_{t})
to
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{apcal}}

10:

Receive

p_{t}
from
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{apcal}}

11:

Send
sample
(x_{t},y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))
to
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{owal}}

12:

Receive

q_{t+1}
from
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{owal}}

Theorem 4.2
.

Given an online sequence of samples
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{T},y_{T})
, a class of loss functions
\mathcal{L}
, a class of hypothesis functions
\mathcal{H}
, an online weak agnostic learner for
\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
, and a failure probability
\delta
,
Algorithm

3
can be initialized to guarantee
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{H})-
online omniprediction
regret with probability at least
1-\delta

O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T/\delta}\right)+\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T)

Proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
.
The online weak agnostic learner with
k
-fold resampling (
Lemma

4.7
) for
k=O(T\log(T/\delta))
guarantees with probability at least
1-\delta

\max_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}c(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}q_{t}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))+\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T)+O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T/\delta}\right)

since
Algorithm

3
sets the labeling function to
r_{t}(x,y)=y-p_{t}(x)
.
The Augmented Proper Calibration algorithm guarantees with probability at least
1-\delta
,
\mathrm{PCalErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T/\delta}\right)
and
\sum_{t=1}^{T}q_{t}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T/\delta}\right)
as stated in
Proposition

3.10
.
The second guarantee is the same as the first term RHS on the online weak agnostic learner guarantee. Thus, we obtain

\max_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}c(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T/\delta}\right)+\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T)

This allows us to conclude proper calibration and
\mathcal{C}
-multiaccuracy, which together implies

(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{OmniRegret}\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T/\delta}\right)+\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T)

∎

Corollary 4.3

(of
Theorems

4.2
and
4.5
)
.

Given a hypothesis class
\mathcal{H}
and a class of loss functions
\mathcal{L}
such that the composed class
\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
has bounded sequential Rademacher complexity, then there exists a forecaster that guarantees expected omniprediction regret bounded by

O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}+\mathsf{srad}_{T}(\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H})\right)

where
\mathsf{srad}_{T}(\mathcal{C})
refers to the sequential Rademacher complexity of a class
\mathcal{C}
.

4.1
Online Weak Agnostic Learning

In this subsection, we discuss the online weak agnostic learning problem in
Definition

4.1
, showing the existence of online weak agnostic learners whose regret scales optimally with the sequential rademacher complexity of the hypothesis class.

First, we note that when the labels are boolean valued and the hypothesis class are constrained to be boolean functions, online weak agnostic learning is equivalent to agnostic online binary classification problem where in each iteration the learner suffers loss of
\mathbf{1}[\hat{y}_{t}\neq y_{t}]
and the goal is to minimize the cummulate loss.

When
\mathcal{C}
is finite, a multiplicative weights algorithm can be used to achieve online weak agnostic learning with regret bounded by
O\left(\sqrt{T\ln|\mathcal{C}|}\right)
.

Lemma 4.4

(Multiplicative Weights)
.

Given a finite hypothesis class
C
,
Algorithm

4
implements an online weak agnostic learner whose regret scales as
O\left(\sqrt{T\ln|\mathcal{C}|}\right)
with probability 1.

Algorithm 4

Multiplicative Weights for Online Weak Agnostic Learning

1:

Input:
Finite hypothesis class
\mathcal{C}
, learning rate
\eta=\sqrt{\frac{\ln|\mathcal{C}|}{T}}

2:

Initialize weights
w_{1}(c)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{C}|}
for all
c\in\mathcal{C}

3:

for

t=1,\ldots,T

do

4:

Receive context
x_{t}

5:

Output prediction
q_{t}(x_{t})=\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}w_{t}(c)c(x_{t})

6:

Observe label
y_{t}\in[-1,1]

7:

Update weights:
w_{t+1}(c)=\frac{w_{t}(c)\exp(\eta c(x_{t})y_{t})}{\sum_{c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}w_{t}(c^{\prime})\exp(\eta c^{\prime}(x_{t})y_{t})}

For infinite hypothesis classes, the achievable regret values is characterized by the sequential Rademacher complexity of the hypothesis class.

Theorem 4.5
.

Given a hypothesis class
C
with finite sequential Rademacher complexity, there exists an algorithm that implements an online weak agnostic learner with regret that scales as
O(T\cdot\mathsf{srad}_{T}(C))
. Moreover, no algorithm can do better than
\Omega(T\cdot\mathsf{srad}_{T}(C))
.

To prove this theorem, we need to formulate the rates for online weak agnostic learning as the minimax value of a game.

Definition 4.2

(Minimax Value of Online Weak Agnostic Learning)
.

Given a hypothesis class
\mathcal{C}\subset\{c:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[-1,1]\}
. Consider randomized learners who predict a distribution
q_{t}\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[-1,1])
and sample
p_{t}
from this distribution on every round
t
. We define the value of the game as

\mathcal{V}_{T}^{\text{owal}}(\mathcal{C})=\inf_{p_{1}\in\mathcal{Q}}\sup_{(x_{1},y_{1})\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}}\mathbb{E}_{p_{1}\sim q_{1}}\cdots\inf_{q_{T}\in\mathcal{Q}}\sup_{(x_{T},y_{T})\in\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}}\mathbb{E}_{p_{T}\sim q_{T}}\left[\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}c(x_{t})y_{t}-\sum_{t=1}^{T}p_{t}(x_{t})y_{t}\right]

(1)

Proof of
Theorem

4.5
.

Since the losses are linear and 1-Lipschitz in the variable
y_{t}
, the upper bound result follows by applying Theorem 8 of
Rakhlin et al., (2015)
to bound the sequential Rademacher complexity of the hypothesis class.
Thus,

\mathcal{V}_{T}^{\text{owal}}(\mathcal{C})\leq 2T\cdot\mathsf{srad}_{T}(C)

To show the lower bound we simply choose the label sequence
y_{1},\ldots,y_{T}
to be i.i.d. Rademacher random variables, thus, it immediately follows that for any sequence
x_{1},\ldots,x_{T}

\mathcal{V}_{T}^{\text{owal}}(\mathcal{C})\geq\E_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{T}}\left[\sup_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}c(x_{t})y_{t}-\sum_{t=1}^{T}p_{t}(x_{t})y_{t}\right]

Since
y_{1},\ldots,y_{T}
are i.i.d. Rademacher random variables, the second term becomes zero in expectation regardless of the choice of
p_{t}
sequence, giving us the desired result.

\mathcal{V}_{T}^{\text{owal}}(\mathcal{C})\geq T\cdot\mathsf{srad}_{T}(C)

∎

Remark 4.6
.

For a finite class
\mathcal{C}
the unnormalized sequential Rademacher complexity satisfies

T\cdot\mathsf{srad}_{T}(\mathcal{C})\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\log|\mathcal{C}|}\right)
,
hence
Theorem

4.5
recovers the same regret bound as

Lemma

4.4
, albeit without supplying an explicit algorithm.

Due to the linearity of the loss function and the fact that the online weak agnostic learner is allowed to output values in the
[-1,1]
interval, there exist
deterministic
online weak agnostic learners for any class
C
that is online learnable. This is because we can always predict the expectation of the
randomized
online weak agnostic learner and achieve the same guarantee. However, such deterministic online weak agnostic learner might be more computationally difficult to implement, so we will only assume access to a
randomized
online weak agnostic learner.

Reducing from Delayed-Label to Simultaneous-Move.

In our omniprediction framework, learning is modeled as an interaction between a randomized online weak agnostic learner and the Augmented Proper Calibration algorithm. At each round
t
, the learner outputs a randomized predictor
Q_{t}\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[-1,1])
, from which a hypothesis
q_{t}\sim Q_{t}
is drawn. This hypothesis is then used by the calibration algorithm to compute a prediction
p_{t}
, which defines the loss function (via
y_{t}
) presented to the learner at that round. Thus, the label at round
t
may depend on one or more realized samples
q_{t}
, and hence the protocol corresponds to a
delayed-label
(DL) model.

However, our definition of an online weak agnostic learner assumes the
simultaneous-move
(SM) protocol: at each round
t
, the learner outputs a randomized distribution
Q_{t}
, and the adversary chooses
(x_{t},y_{t})
without knowledge of the specific realization
q_{t}\sim Q_{t}
. This discrepancy between the learner’s guarantee and its downstream usage necessitates a reduction from the DL to the SM model. To achieve this, we invoke a
k
-fold resampling
technique: at each round
t
, we independently sample
k=O(T\log(T/\delta))
hypotheses
q^{1}_{t},\ldots,q^{k}_{t}\sim Q_{t}
and use the empirical average
\bar{q}_{t}:=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}q^{i}_{t}
as the predictor used to define the loss.

This transformation enables a high-probability guarantee even under the stronger DL adversary model:

Lemma 4.7

(High-Probability Guarantee via
k
-Fold Resampling)
.

Let
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{T}\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[-1,1])
be the randomized predictors computed by an online weak agnostic learner satisfying the following regret bound with probability
1-\delta/2
under the simultaneous-move protocol:

\max_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}c(x_{t})y_{t}\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\E_{q_{t}\sim Q_{t}}[q_{t}(x_{t})y_{t}]+\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T).

Suppose that at each round
t
, we sample
k=O(T\log(T/\delta))
independent hypotheses
q^{1}_{t},\ldots,q^{k}_{t}\sim Q_{t}
and define the empirical predictor
\bar{q}_{t}:=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}q^{i}_{t}
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\max_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}c(x_{t})y_{t}\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bar{q}_{t}(x_{t})y_{t}+\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T)+\sqrt{T\log(T/\delta)}.

Proof of
Lemma

4.7
.

Since
x_{t}
does not depend on the realizations
q^{1}_{t},\ldots,q^{k}_{t}\sim Q_{t}
we have

\E[q^{i}_{t}(x_{t})]=\E_{q_{t}\sim Q_{t}}[q_{t}(x_{t})]
for
i=1,\ldots,k
. By Hoeffding’s inequality,

\Pr\left(\big|\bar{q}_{t}(x_{t})-\E_{q_{t}\sim Q_{t}}[q_{t}(x_{t})]\big|\,>\,\sqrt{\frac{\log(T/\delta)}{T}}\right)\;<\;\frac{\delta}{2T}.

Using a union bound over
T
rounds, along with the fact that
-1\leq y_{t}\leq 1
, we have that with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\bar{q}_{t}(x_{t})y_{t}-\E_{q_{t}\sim Q_{t}}[q_{t}(x_{t})y_{t}]\right)\right|\leq\sqrt{T\log(T/\delta)}.

Combining this with the high-probability regret bound under the SM protocol completes the proof.
∎

Online Weak Agnostic Learning for
\{-1,+1\}\times\mathcal{C}
.

Our algorithms for omniprediction will actually use an online weak agnostic learning oracle for
\{-1,+1\}\times\mathcal{C}
. However, one should think of this as equivalent to
\mathcal{C}
, because of the following lemma.

Lemma 4.8
.

Given an online weak agnostic learner for
\mathcal{C}
, one can construct an online weak agnostic learner for
\{-1,+1\}\times\mathcal{C}
that uses two calls to the original learner for
\mathcal{C}
at each round. The resulting algorithm’s regret satisfies

\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\{-1,+1\}\times\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T)\;\leq\;\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T)\;+\;O\bigl(\sqrt{T}\bigr).

Proof.

The main idea is as follows: we run two copies of the online weak agnostic learner for
\mathcal{C}
, one with the labels unchanged (“positive sign”) and one with the labels negated (“negative sign”), and then apply multiplicative weights to combine their predictions.

Concretely, let
{\textsc{\large owal}}^{+}
be the online weak agnostic learner run on the sequence
\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{t-1},y_{t-1})\}
, and let
{\textsc{\large owal}}^{-}
be another instance run on
\{(x_{1},-y_{1}),\dots,(x_{t-1},-y_{t-1})\}
. In each round
t
:

1.

We query
{\textsc{\large owal}}^{+}
to get a predictor
q^{+}_{t}
and query
{\textsc{\large owal}}^{-}
to get a predictor
q^{-}_{t}
.

2.

We maintain an exponential-weight “score” for each of the two experts, reflecting their cumulative performance so far. Specifically, we use a learning rate
\eta=T^{-1/2}
, and multiply the weight of the positive-sign expert by
\exp\bigl(\eta\sum_{u=1}^{t-1}q^{+}_{u}(x_{u})\,y_{u}\bigr)
, whereas the weight of the negative-sign expert is multiplied by
\exp\bigl(-\eta\sum_{u=1}^{t-1}q^{-}_{u}(x_{u})\,y_{u}\bigr)
.

3.

We normalize these two weights and form a convex combination:

q_{t}\;=\;\alpha_{t}\,q^{+}_{t}\;+\;(1-\alpha_{t})\,q^{-}_{t},

where

\alpha_{t}\;=\;\frac{\exp\bigl(\eta\,\sum_{u=1}^{t-1}q^{+}_{u}(x_{u})\,y_{u}\bigr)}{\exp\bigl(\eta\,\sum_{u=1}^{t-1}q^{+}_{u}(x_{u})\,y_{u}\bigr)\;+\;\exp\bigl(-\eta\,\sum_{u=1}^{t-1}q^{-}_{u}(x_{u})\,y_{u}\bigr)}.

4.

We output the single deterministic predictior
q_{t}
for round
t
, and after seeing
y_{t}
, update the two experts’ weights accordingly.

By the usual analysis of multiplicative weights, the combination’s cumulative reward
\sum_{t=1}^{T}q_{t}\,y_{t}
is within
O(\sqrt{T})
of the better of the two experts (i.e.,
{\textsc{\large owal}}^{+}
or
{\textsc{\large owal}}^{-}
). Moreover, each of these two experts has online weak agnostic regret at most
\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T)
relative to the best function in
\mathcal{C}
with sign fixed. Thus, we obtain

\sum_{t=1}^{T}q_{t}(x_{t})\,y_{t}\;\;\geq\;\;\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\{+,-\}\\
c\in\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}s\,c(x_{t})\,y_{t}\;-\;\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\delta}(T)\;-\;O\bigl(\sqrt{T}\bigr).

Rearranging yields the stated bound on
\mathrm{OracleReg}^{\delta}_{\{-1,+1\}\times\mathcal{C}}(T)
.
∎

4.2
Omniprediction for Finite Classes

As an easy first application of
Theorem

4.2
, we assume the loss class
\mathcal{L}
and hypothesis class

\mathcal{H}
are both finite, and we obtain online omnipredictors whose regret depends near-optimally on
T
and only
logarithmically on
|\mathcal{L}|
and
|\mathcal{H}|
.

Corollary 4.9

(Omniprediction for Finite Loss Classes)
.

Given a finite class of hypothesis functions
\mathcal{H}
and a finite class of bounded loss functions
\mathcal{L}
,
Algorithm

3
guarantees expected omniprediction regret of
O\left(\sqrt{T\ln(|\mathcal{H}|\cdot|\mathcal{L}|\cdot T)}\right)
using
Algorithm

4
as the online weak agnostic learning oracle.

The proof of the corollary is immediate by combining
Theorem

4.2
with
Lemma

4.4
and observing
that the cardinality of the class
\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
is bounded above by
|\mathcal{H}|\cdot|\mathcal{L}|
.

4.3
Omniprediction for Infinite Loss Classes with Finite Approximate Basis

While achieving omniprediction for infinite loss classes might seem challenging, a key observation is that if a class of loss functions
\mathcal{F}

can be approximated by a simpler set of “basis functions”
\mathcal{G}
, then multiaccuracy for
\Delta\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{H}

implies multiaccuracy for
\Delta\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{H}
with bounded excess regret. In the sequel, we will apply this abstract
approximation result, taking our basis functions to be thresholds or ReLUs (or finite subsets thereof) and using them to approximate
important infinite classes of losses.

Definition 4.3

(Approximate Basis)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a set and
\mathcal{F}=\{f:\Gamma\rightarrow[-1,1]\}
a class of functions on
\Gamma
.
We say that a set
\mathcal{G}=\{g:\Gamma\rightarrow[-1,1]\}

is an
\varepsilon
-basis for
\mathcal{F}
with sparsity
d
and coefficient norm
\lambda
, if for every function
f\in\mathcal{F}
,
there exists a finite subset
\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{d}\}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
and
coefficients
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{d}\in[-1,1]

satisfying

\forall x\in\Gamma\;\;\left|f(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{d}c_{i}g_{i}(x)\right|\leq\varepsilon\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{d}|c_{i}|\leq\lambda.

(37)

In the special case when
\mathcal{G}
itself has
d
elements, we say

\mathcal{G}
is a
finite
\varepsilon
-basis for
\mathcal{F}
of
size

d
and coefficient norm
\lambda
.

A useful property of approximate bases is that the approximation is preserved under
post-composition with any class of functions.

Lemma 4.10

(Finite approximate bases are preserved under post-composition)
.

Let
\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}
be sets and
\mathcal{H}=\{h:\Gamma_{0}\to\Gamma_{1}\}
a
class of functions from
\Gamma_{0}
to
\Gamma_{1}
.
If
\mathcal{F}=\{f:\Gamma_{1}\rightarrow[-1,1]\}
is any class of functions on
\Gamma_{1}
and

\mathcal{G}=\{g:\Gamma_{1}\rightarrow[-1,1]\}
is an
\varepsilon
-basis
for
\mathcal{F}
with sparsity
d
and coefficient norm
\lambda
, then

\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{H}
is an
\varepsilon
-basis for
\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{H}

with sparsity
d
and coefficient norm
\lambda
.

Proof.

Consider any
f\in\mathcal{F}
.
If coefficients
c_{1},\ldots,c_{d}
satisfy property (
37
)
in the definition of
\varepsilon
-basis with coefficient norm
\lambda
, then
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
, we have

\forall x\in\Gamma_{0}\;\;\left|f(h(x))-\sum_{i=1}^{d}c_{i}g_{i}(h(x))\right|\leq\varepsilon\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{d}|c_{i}|\leq\lambda.

which confirms that
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{H}
is an
\varepsilon
-basis for

\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{H}
with coefficient norm 1.
∎

Approximate bases allow us to extend multiaccuracy from the basis functions to the entire class
\mathcal{F}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\mathcal{F}=\{f:[0,1]\rightarrow[-1,1]\}
be a class of functions
and
\mathcal{G}=\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{d}:[0,1]\rightarrow[-1,1]\}
an

\varepsilon
-basis for
\mathcal{F}
with sparsity
d
and coefficient norm
\lambda
.
Then for any sequence of
T
predictions
\mathbf{p}
and context, outcome pairs
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}

\mathcal{F}\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\lambda\cdot\mathcal{G}\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+\varepsilon T

Similarly, in the distributional setting, for any distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathcal{X}\times[0,1]
and any predictor
p:\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
,

\mathcal{F}\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}_{\mathcal{D}}(p)\leq\lambda\cdot\mathcal{G}\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}_{\mathcal{D}}(p)+\varepsilon.

Proof.

We can write every
f\in\mathcal{F}
as follows:
f(z)=\sum_{i\in[d]}c_{i}(f)g_{i}(z)+\varepsilon(f)
such that
\sum_{i\in[d]}|c_{i}(f)|\leq\lambda
,
|\varepsilon(f)|\leq\varepsilon
and
g_{1},\ldots g_{d}\in\mathcal{G}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}f(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|

\displaystyle=\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\sum_{i\in[d]}c_{i}(f)g_{i}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right)+\varepsilon(f)(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|

(38)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\sum_{i\in[d]}c_{i}(f)\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}g_{i}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right)\right|+\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\varepsilon(f)(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|

(39)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{i\in[d]}|c_{i}(f)|\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}g_{i}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|+\varepsilon T

(since
|\varepsilon(f)|\leq\varepsilon
)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left(\sum_{i\in[d]}|c_{i}(f)|\right)\max_{i\in[d]}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}g_{i}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|+\varepsilon T

(40)

\displaystyle=\lambda\max_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{basis}}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}g(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|+\varepsilon T

(41)

The bound for multiaccuracy error in the distributional setting follows by an identical calculation,
substituting expectations over
\mathcal{D}
for sums over
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
.
∎

To illustrate the application of
Lemma

4.11
, we note the following consequence for
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{H})
-online omniprediction when

|\mathcal{H}|<\infty
and
\Delta\mathcal{L}
has a finite approximate basis.

Proposition 4.12

(Omniprediction for Infinite Loss Classes and Finite Hypothesis Classes)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite class of hypothesis functions. Let
\mathcal{L}
be a (possibly infinite) class of loss functions whose discrete derivative class
\Delta\mathcal{L}
admits a finite
\varepsilon
-basis of size
d
with coefficient norm
\lambda
. There exists a forecaster that guarantees
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{OmniRegret}\leq O\left(\lambda\sqrt{T\ln(|\mathcal{H}|\cdot dT)}+\varepsilon T\right)
.

Proof of
Proposition

4.12
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
denote the finite
\varepsilon
-basis of
\Delta\mathcal{L}
of size
d
and coefficient norm
\lambda
.
By
Lemma

4.10
,
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{H}
is an
\varepsilon
-basis of

\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
with the same sparsity and coefficient norm.
We run
Algorithm

3
with hypothesis
class
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{H}
instead of
\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
.

By
Corollary

4.9
, this algorithm guarantees expected proper calibration error and expected
(\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{H})
-multiaccuracy error of
O\left(\sqrt{T\ln(|\mathcal{H}|\cdot dT)}\right)
.

Now, we use
Lemma

4.11
to bound the
(\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H})
-multiaccuracy error in terms of the
(\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{H})
-multiaccuracy error.

(\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}\leq\lambda(\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+\varepsilon T

Combining these bounds gives the desired bound of
O\left(\lambda\sqrt{T\ln(|\mathcal{H}|\cdot dT)}+\varepsilon T\right)
on the
(\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H})
-multiaccuracy error of the forecaster.
∎

Proposition 4.13

(Omniprediction for Infinite Loss and Hypothesis Classes)
.

For any loss class
\mathcal{L}
and hypothesis class
\mathcal{H}
, if we are given an online weak agnostic learning
oracle for
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{H}
where
\mathcal{G}
is an
\varepsilon
-basis of
\Delta\mathcal{L}\circ\mathcal{H}
with
coefficient norm
\lambda
, then the application of
Algorithm

3
with hypothesis class

\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{H}
yields an omnipredictor with

(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{OmniRegret}\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}+\varepsilon T+\lambda\cdot\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{H}}(T)\right)
.

Proof.

The regret bound is a direct application of
Theorem

4.2
combined with
Lemma

4.11
.
∎

In
Section

6.1
, we will need to make use of a generalization of

Definition

4.3
that allows approximating functions in
\mathcal{F}
using

infinite
linear combinations of elements of the function class
\mathcal{G}
.
For future reference, we provide the generalization here.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
\Gamma
be a set and
\mathcal{F}=\{f:\Gamma\to[-1,1]\}
a
class of functions on
\Gamma
. Let
(\Omega,\Sigma)
be a measurable space and

\mathcal{G}=\left\{g_{\omega}:\Gamma\to[-1,1]\,\mid\,\omega\in\Omega\right\}

a class of functions on
\Gamma
indexed by
\Omega
.
We say
\mathcal{F}
is
(\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
-spanned with
coefficient norm
\lambda
if for every
f\in\mathcal{F}

there is a signed measure
c_{f}
on
(\Omega,\Sigma)

such that
\|c_{f}\|=|c_{f}|(\Omega)\leq\lambda
and

\forall x\in\Gamma\quad\left|f(x)-\int_{\Omega}g_{\omega}(x)\,\mathrm{d}c_{f}(\omega)\right|\leq\varepsilon.

Lemma

4.11
generalizes to

(\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
-spanned classes under this definition,
as follows.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
(\Omega,\Sigma)
be a measurable space and
and
\mathcal{G}=\left\{g_{\omega}:\Gamma\rightarrow[0,1]\;\mid\;\omega\in\Omega\right\}

a class of functions indexed by
\Omega
.
Suppose
\mathcal{F}=\{f:[0,1]\rightarrow[-1,1]\}

is a class of functions that is

(\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
-spanned with coefficient norm
\lambda
.
Then for any sequence of
T
predictions
\mathbf{p}
and context, outcome pairs
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}

\mathcal{F}\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\lambda\cdot\mathcal{G}\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})+\varepsilon T

Similarly, in the distributional setting, for any distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathcal{X}\times[0,1]
and any predictor
p:\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
,

\mathcal{F}\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}_{\mathcal{D}}(p)\leq\lambda\cdot\mathcal{G}\text{-}\mathrm{MAErr}_{\mathcal{D}}(p)+\varepsilon.

Proof.

We can write every
f\in\mathcal{F}
as follows:
f(z)=\int_{\Omega}g_{\omega}(z)\,\mathrm{d}c_{f}(\omega)+\varepsilon_{f}(z)
such that
|c|(\Omega)\leq\lambda
,
\|\varepsilon_{f}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
and
g_{\omega}\in\mathcal{G}
for all
\omega\in\Omega
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}f(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\int_{\Omega}g_{\omega}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\,\mathrm{d}c_{f}(\omega)\right)+\varepsilon_{f}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|

(42)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left\{\,\left|\int_{\Omega}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}g_{\omega}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right)\,\mathrm{d}c_{f}(\omega)\right|+\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\varepsilon_{f}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|\,\right\}

(43)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\int_{\Omega}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}g_{\omega}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|\,\mathrm{d}c_{f}(\omega)\;+\;\varepsilon T

(since
|\varepsilon_{f}(x_{t})|\leq\varepsilon,\,|y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t})|\leq 1
)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\|c_{f}\|\cdot\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}g_{\omega}(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|\;+\;\varepsilon T

(44)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}g(x_{t})(y_{t}-p_{t}(x_{t}))\right|+\varepsilon T

(45)

The bound for multiaccuracy error in the distributional setting follows by an identical calculation,
substituting expectations over
\mathcal{D}
for sums over
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
.

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)(y-p(x))\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(\int_{\Omega}g_{\omega}(x)(y-p(x))\,\mathrm{d}c_{f}(\omega)\right)+\varepsilon_{f}(x)(y-p(x))\right]\,\right|

(46)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left\{\,\left|\int_{\Omega}\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[g_{\omega}(x)(y-p(x))\right]\,\mathrm{d}c_{f}(\omega)\right|+\left|\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\varepsilon_{f}(x)(y-p(x))\right]\,\right|\,\right\}

(47)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\int_{\Omega}\left|\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[g_{\omega}(x)(y-p(x))\right]\,\right|\,\mathrm{d}c_{f}(\omega)\;+\;\varepsilon

(since
|\varepsilon_{f}(x)|\leq\varepsilon,|y-p(x)|\leq 1
)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\|c_{f}\|\cdot\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}\left|\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[g_{\omega}(x)(y-p(x))\right]\,\right|\;+\;\varepsilon

(48)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\left|\E_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[g(x)(y-p(x))\right]\,\right|+\varepsilon

(49)

∎

5
Omniprediction for Notable Loss Classes

This section instantiates
Proposition

4.12
for concrete classes of losses,
by exhibiting approximate bases with appropriate parameters. In particular, we focus on the classes of
all
1
-Lipschitz convex losses (
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cvx}}
), all 1-Lipschitz
losses (
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{lip}}
), and all bounded variation losses (
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}
)
including proper losses (
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}
).
The bases we develop in this section will also be used in developing efficient offline algorithms for omniprediction with respect to these infinite classes.

5.1
Online Omniprediction bounds for Notable Loss Classes

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=\{h:\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]\}
be a hypothesis class.

1.

Given an online weak agnostic learning oracle
for
\mathrm{ReLU}^{1/T}\circ\mathcal{H}
,
Algorithm

3
implements an online omnipredictor for
loss class
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cvx}}
with regret

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cvx}},\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{OmniRegret}\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}+\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathrm{ReLU}^{1/T}\circ\mathcal{H}}(T)\right).

(50)

2.

Given an online weak agnostic learning oracle
for
\mathrm{Th}^{1/T}\circ\mathcal{H}
,
Algorithm

3
implements an online omnipredictor for
loss class
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{lip}}
with regret

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{lip}},\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{OmniRegret}\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}+\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathrm{\mathrm{Th}^{1/T}}\circ\mathcal{H}}(T)\right).

(51)

3.

Suppose the hypotheses in
\mathcal{H}
are
\Gamma
-valued, where
\Gamma
is a finite subset of
[0,1]

containing
\{0,1\}
. Given an online weak agnostic learning
oracle for
\mathrm{Th}^{\Gamma}\circ\mathcal{H}
,
Algorithm

3
implements an online omnipredictor for
loss class
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}
with regret

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}},\mathcal{H})\text{-}\mathrm{OmniRegret}\leq O\left(\sqrt{T\ln T}+\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathrm{\mathrm{Th}^{\Gamma}}\circ\mathcal{H}}(T)\right).

(52)

If
\mathcal{H}
is a finite hypothesis class, then the omniprediction bounds in parts
1

and
2
above are
O\left(\sqrt{T\ln(|\mathcal{H}|\cdot T)}\right)
, and the bound in part
3

is
O\left(\sqrt{T\ln(|\Gamma|\cdot|\mathcal{H}|\cdot T)}\right)
.

Proof.

The bounds (
50
), (
51
), (
52
)
follow directly from application of
Theorem

4.2
with

Corollary

5.3
,
Lemma

5.4
, and
Lemma

5.7
,
respectively. The bounds for finite hypothesis classes follow
by applying
Proposition

4.12
.
∎

To aid in interpreting the regret bounds (
50
)-(
52
), we remind the reader
that for any class
\mathcal{C}
there exists a (not necessarily computationally efficient) online
weak agnostic learning oracle satisfying
\mathrm{OracleReg}_{\mathcal{C}}(T)=O(T\cdot\mathsf{srad}_{T}(\mathcal{C}))
,
where
\mathsf{srad}
denotes sequential Rademacher complexity.

5.2
Approximate Bases for Notable Loss Classes

In this subsection we exhibit approximate bases for the loss classes listed above.
The quantitative consequences for omniprediction will be detailed in the following section.

Convex Lipschitz Loss Functions.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{cvx}}
be the class of all convex 1-Lipschitz functions.

Lemma 5.2

(
Gopalan et al., 2024b ()
)
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{cvx}}
admits a finite
\varepsilon
-basis of ReLU functions of size
\tilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2/3})
with coefficient norm
2
.

Convex functions are not closed under linear combinations. This means that not all functions in
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cvx}}
will be convex, therefore we cannot apply the result from section above.
However, the fact that this class is derived from the difference of two convex functions still allows us to derive useful upper bounds.

Corollary 5.3

(of
Lemma

5.2
)
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
,
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cvx}}
admits a finite
\varepsilon
-basis of ReLU functions of size
\tilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2/3})
with coefficient norm
4
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathrm{ReLU}
be a finite
\varepsilon/2
-basis for
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{cvx}}
of size
\tilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2/3})
with coefficient norm
2
.
Denote the elements of
\mathcal{G}
as
g_{1},\ldots,g_{d}
where
d=\tilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2/3})
.
If
f\in\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cvx}}
then
f(x)=f_{1}(x)-f_{0}(x)
where
f_{0},f_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{cvx}}
. Let
c_{0i},c_{1i}\;(1\leq i\leq d)

be coefficients such that
\|f_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{d}c_{0i}g_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/2,

\|f_{1}-\sum_{i=1}^{d}c_{1i}g_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/2,

\sum_{i=1}^{d}|c_{0i}|\leq 2,
and
\sum_{i=1}^{d}|c_{1i}|\leq 2.

Then for the coefficients
c_{i}=c_{1i}-c_{0i}
we have

\displaystyle\left\|f-\sum_{i=1}^{d}c_{i}g_{i}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\|(f_{1}-f_{0})-\sum_{i=1}^{d}(c_{1i}-c_{0i})g_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|f_{1}-\sum_{i=1}^{d}c_{1i}g_{i}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|f_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{d}c_{0i}g_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}|c_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}|c_{0i}|+\sum_{i=1}^{d}|c_{1i}|\leq 4,

which confirms that
\mathcal{G}
is a finite
\varepsilon
-basis of ReLU functions for
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cvx}}
of size
\tilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2/3})
and coefficient norm
4
.
∎

Lipschitz Loss Functions.

Every 1-Lipschitz loss function can be
\varepsilon
-approximated by a finite weighted sum of
ReLU functions, but the coefficient norm of this approximation is not bounded by a constant (independent of
\varepsilon
). To
achieve approximation with bounded coefficient norm, it is necessary to use threshold functions.

Definition 5.1
.

For
\theta\in[0,1]
let
\mathrm{Th}_{\theta}
denote the
\{\pm 1\}
-valued
threshold function
\mathrm{Th}_{\theta}(v)=\mathrm{sgn}(v-\theta),
with the
convention that
\mathrm{Th}_{\theta}(\theta)=1
.
For a subset
\Gamma\subseteq[0,1]
let
\mathrm{Th}^{\Gamma}=\{\mathrm{Th}_{\theta}\,|\,\theta\in\Gamma\}
.
When
\Gamma(\varepsilon)
is the set of integer multiples of
\varepsilon
in
[0,1]
, we will abbreviate

\mathrm{Th}^{\Gamma(\varepsilon)}
as
\mathrm{Th}^{\varepsilon}
. We will also abbreviate

\mathrm{Th}^{[0,1]}
as
\mathrm{Th}
.

Lemma 5.4

(Threshold Basis for Lipschitz Functions)
.

For the class
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{lip}}
of 1-Lipschitz loss functions, the class
\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{Th}^{\varepsilon/2}
is a
finite
\varepsilon
-basis for
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{lip}}
of size
\lceil\frac{2}{\varepsilon}+1\rceil
and coefficient norm 4.

Proof of
Lemma

5.4
.

To simplify notation we will prove
\mathrm{Th}^{\varepsilon}
is a

2\varepsilon
-basis for
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{lip}}
of size
\lceil\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+1\rceil
and coefficient norm 2.
(The lemma follows by reinterpreting
\varepsilon
in this proof as
\varepsilon/2
in the lemma statement.)
For
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{lip}}
the function
f(p)=\Delta\ell(p)=\ell(p,1)-\ell(p,0)
is 2-Lipschitz and
[-1,1]
-valued.
We can construct a piecewise constant function
\hat{f}
by setting
\hat{f}(x)=f\left(\lfloor\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\rfloor\cdot\varepsilon\right)
so that
|f(x)-\hat{f}(x)|\leq 2\varepsilon
. We’ll now express
\hat{f}
as a linear combination of functions in
\mathrm{Th}^{\varepsilon}
. Let
g_{i}(v)
denote the function
\mathrm{Th}_{i\varepsilon}\in\mathrm{Th}^{\varepsilon}
.

\hat{f}(x)=\hat{f}(0)g_{0}(x)+\sum_{i\in[\lceil 1/\varepsilon\rceil]}(\hat{f}(i\varepsilon)-\hat{f}((i-1)\varepsilon))g_{i}(x).

To see this, observe that we can simplify the RHS to

\hat{f}(0)(g_{0}(x)-g_{1}(x))+\sum_{i\in[\lceil 1/\varepsilon\rceil]}\hat{f}(i\varepsilon)(g_{i}(x)-g_{i+1}(x))

Since
(g_{i}(x)-g_{i+1}(x))
is 1 only when
i\varepsilon\leq x<(i+1)\varepsilon
and
\hat{f}(x)
is constant in this interval, we get the desired result.
Now we need to bound the coefficient norm. We know that
|\hat{f}(0)|\leq 1
and since
f
is 1-lipschitz,
|(\hat{f}(i\varepsilon)-\hat{f}((i-1)\varepsilon))|\leq\varepsilon
for all
i
. Thus, the coefficient norm is bounded by 2.
∎

Bounded Variation Losses and Proper Losses.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}
denote the class of bounded variation losses, defined as follows.

Definition 5.2

(Bounded Variation)
.

A function
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
has
bounded variation
if the quantity

V(f)=\sup\left\{\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}|f(x_{i})-f(x_{i-1})|\;\right|0=x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{n}=1\right\}

is finite. The class
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}
of bounded variation losses consists of all loss functions
\ell(p,y)
taking values in
[-1,1]

that satisfy
V(\Delta\ell)\leq 2
.

Bounded variation losses are an extremely general family that includes all other loss
classes considered in this paper.

Lemma 5.5
.

The class
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}
includes all 1-Lipschitz losses,
convex losses (with values in
[-1/4,\,1/4]
,
regardless of whether or not they are Lipschitz continuous),
and proper losses taking values in
[-1,1]
.

Proof.

It is clear from the definition that
V(f)\leq 2
when

f
is monotone and
[-1,1]
-valued, and also when
f
is 2-Lipschitz.
Hence, the class
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{lip}}
of 1-Lipschitz losses is in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}
(since

\Delta\ell
is 2-Lipschitz whenever
\ell
is 1-Lipschitz) and
the class
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}
of proper losses is in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}
(since
\Delta\ell

is monotone whenever
\ell
is proper). For a convex function

f
taking values in
[-1/4,\,1/4]
, if
x^{*}
denotes a point in
[0,1]
where
f
attains its
minimum, then
f
admits a representation of the form

f(x)=f(x^{*})+f_{0}(x)+f_{1}(x)

where
f_{0},f_{1}
are monotone non-increasing and non-decreasing
functions (respectively) from
[0,1]
to
[0,1/2]
satisfying

f_{0}(x)=0
for
x\geq x^{*}
and
f_{1}(x)=0
for
x\leq x^{*}
.
From this representation it is clear that
V(f)\leq V(f_{0})+V(f_{1})\leq 1.

If
\ell(p,y)
is a convex loss taking values in
[-1/4,\,1/4]
then

\Delta\ell
is a difference of two convex
[-1/4,\,1/4]
-valued
functions, so
V(\Delta\ell)\leq 2.

∎

For bounded variation losses we have the following approximate basis.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, the class
\mathrm{Th}
of all threshold functions on
[0,1]

is a
\varepsilon
-basis for
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}
with sparsity
\lceil 2/\varepsilon+1\rceil

and coefficient norm 3.

Proof.

For
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}
let
f=\Delta\ell
, and consider the
sequence
x_{0}<x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{n}
defined inductively
by setting
x_{0}=0
and
x_{m+1}=\inf\{x>x_{m}\,:\,|f(x)-f(x_{m})|>\varepsilon\}

for all
m\geq 0
such that the set in question is non-empty.
The sequence ends with the first element

x_{n}
such that
\{x>x_{n}\,:\,|f(x)-f(x_{n})|>\varepsilon\}

is empty. For notational convenience we define
x_{n+1}=1
.
From the definition of
V(f)
we see that

V(f)\geq n\varepsilon
, from which we deduce
n\leq 2/\varepsilon
.

Define a piecewise-constant function
\hat{f}:[0,1]\to[-1,1]

by setting
\hat{f}(x)=f(x_{m})
where
x_{m}
is the
maximum element of
\{x_{0},\ldots,x_{n+1}\}\cap[0,x]
.
By construction, the inequality
|f(x)-\hat{f}(x)|\leq\varepsilon

holds for all
x\in[x_{m},x_{m+1}]
, for all
m\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
.
The union of the intervals
[x_{m},x_{m+1}]
equals
[0,1]
, so

\|f-\hat{f}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
.

For
i=0,1,\ldots,n
, let
g_{i}
denote the threshold function

\mathrm{Th}_{x_{i}}
. As in the proof of
Lemma

5.4

we have

\hat{f}(x)=\hat{f}(0)g_{0}(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\left(\hat{f}(x_{i})-\hat{f}(x_{i-1})\right)g_{i}(x).

The coefficient norm is bounded by
|\hat{f}(0)|+\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}|\hat{f}(x_{i})-\hat{f}(x_{i-1}|\leq 1+V(f)\leq 3.

∎

The approximate basis
\mathrm{Th}
is unfortunately not finite.
This difficulty is inherent: the class
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}
includes,
for each
\theta\in(0,1)
, a step function with a step of height 1
at
\theta
. To approximate such a step function within
\varepsilon
in
the
\infty
-norm, one must use a function having a jump discontinuity
at
\theta
, provided
\varepsilon<1/2
. Hence, any
\varepsilon
-approximate
basis for
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{proper}}
must include functions with jump
discontinuities at every
\theta\in(0,1)
.
However, this difficulty can be overcome for the class

\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}\circ\mathcal{H}
whenever
\mathcal{H}
is a class of hypothesis functions
taking values in a finite subset of
[0,1]
.

Lemma 5.7
.

If
\mathcal{H}
is a class of hypothesis functions
taking values in a finite set
\Gamma
with

\{0,1\}\subseteq\Gamma\subset[0,1]
,
then for all
\varepsilon>0
the class

\mathrm{Th}^{\Gamma}\circ\mathcal{H}
is a

\varepsilon
-basis for
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}\circ\mathcal{H}

with sparsity
\lceil 2/\varepsilon+1\rceil

and coefficient norm 3.

Proof.

From
Lemma

4.10
and
Lemma

5.6

we know that
\mathrm{Th}\circ\mathcal{H}
is a

\varepsilon
-basis for
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}\circ\mathcal{H}

with sparsity
\lceil 2/\varepsilon+1\rceil

and coefficient norm 3. However, for every

\theta\in[0,1]
, if
\gamma
is the
minimum element of
\Gamma\cap[\theta,1]

then
\mathrm{Th}_{\theta}\circ h=\mathrm{Th}_{\gamma}\circ h
for every

h\in\mathcal{H}
. Hence,
\mathrm{Th}^{\Gamma}\circ\mathcal{H}=\mathrm{Th}\circ\mathcal{H}
and the lemma follows.
∎

Corollary 5.8

(of
Lemma

5.6
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a class of hypothesis functions. Then

\mathsf{rad}_{m}(\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}\circ\mathcal{H})=O(\mathsf{rad}_{m}(\mathrm{Th}\circ\mathcal{H}))

Proof.

By Lemma
5.6
, for any
\epsilon>0
, the class
\mathrm{Th}
of threshold functions is an
\epsilon
-basis for
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}
with sparsity
s=O(1/\epsilon)
and coefficient norm at most 3. This means every
g\in\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}
can be written as
g(\cdot)=\sum_{j=1}^{s}\alpha_{j}t_{j}(\cdot)
where each
t_{j}\in\mathrm{Th}
.
By the definition of Rademacher complexity,

\mathsf{rad}_{m}(\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}\circ\mathcal{H})=\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{f\in\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}\circ\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}f(x_{i})\right].

Substituting the basis representation of
f
, we obtain

\sup_{f\in\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BV}}\circ\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}f(x_{i})=\sup_{\|\alpha\|_{1}\leq 3}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{s}\alpha_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}t_{j}(h(x_{i})).
